Observational Study 1 Medicine ® The relationship between problematic social media use and psychological factors among university students with evaluation of ADHD, social anxiety and self-esteem Gamze Gürcan, MD, MSca,* , Latife Büşra Çokaslan, MDa Abstract Problematic social media use (PSMU) has been increasingly associated with various psychological vulnerabilities; however, findings remain mixed, particularly among university students and in non-Western contexts. This study aimed to examine the associations between PSMU and psychological factors including attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) symptoms, social anxiety, and self-esteem among university students, as well as to explore the role of selected sociodemographic and usagerelated characteristics. This cross-sectional study included 179 university students aged 18 to 25 years. Participants completed a sociodemographic and clinical information form, the Social Media Addiction Scale–Adult Form, Adult Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder Self-Report Scale, Liebowitz Social Anxiety Scale, and Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale–Short Form. Statistical analyses were conducted using SPSS v22.0. Spearman correlation analyses and group comparisons were performed, with statistical significance set at P < .05. Mean daily screen time was 352.29 ± 156.41 minutes, significantly higher than the global average. Social media addiction scale scores positively correlated with ADHD symptoms (r = 0.412, P < .001), social anxiety (r = 0.395, P < .001), and low self-esteem (r = 0.221, P = .03). Participants who found it easier to communicate on social media exhibited higher social anxiety scores. The use of social media with hidden identities or fake accounts was associated with increased screen time, social media addiction severity, and social anxiety. The findings indicate that problematic social media use among university students is closely associated with psychological vulnerabilities, particularly ADHD symptoms and social anxiety, whereas the role of self-esteem appears more nuanced. These results support the conceptualization of PSMU as a multidimensional phenomenon influenced by individual psychological traits and usage-related behaviors rather than by time spent online alone. By providing data from a non-Western university student population, this study contributes to the cultural generalizability of existing literature and highlights the importance of considering multiple psychological domains when addressing problematic social media use. Keywords: ADHD, PSMU, self-esteem, social anxiety, social media 1. Introduction The exponential growth of digital technologies has led to a marked increase in global internet usage, with over 62.6% of the population actively engaging with social media plat forms as of May 2024.[1,2] Despite its ubiquity, the empirical literature on problematic social media use (PSMU) remains underdeveloped relative to broader research on internet and smartphone addiction. Internet addiction presents in various subtypes, including social media addiction, gaming disorder, cybersex addiction, and compulsive online shopping.[3] While the detrimental effects – such as social withdrawal, financial distress, familial conflict, and deviant behavior in minors – are well documented, the nosological status of internet-related disorders remains contentious. Competing paradigms situate these behaviors within the frameworks of substance use dis orders, obsessive-compulsive spectrum disorders, or impulse control disorders, reflecting significant diagnostic ambiguity.[4,5] Consequently, terminological inconsistency persists, with over lapping labels such as “problematic internet use,” “internet addiction and “internetomania.”[6,7] The authors have no funding and conflicts of interest to declare. © 2026 Nigerian Postgraduate Medical Journal | Published by Wolters Kluwer - Medknow 496 Original Article Introduction Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) is the most commonly identified childhood neurodevelopmental disease.[1] The characteristics of ADHD are problems such as lack of attention, hyperactivity and impulsiveness.[2] Inattention, impulsivity and hyperactivity are three main symptoms seen in a child who has ADHD. According to What Predicts Attention‐Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder Literacy in Primary Schoolteachers? Jemy J. Jome, Vidya Prabhu, Varalakshmi Chandra Sekaran, R. Vani Lakshmi1 Departments of Global Public Health Policy and Governance and 1Applied Statistics and Data Sciences, Prasanna School of Public Health, Manipal Academy of Higher Education, Manipal, Karnataka, India Background: Attention‐Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD) is a common neurodevelopmental condition affecting children’s learning and behaviour. Teachers play a pivotal role in early identification, yet little is known about factors predicting their ADHD literacy in India. Aims: This study was conducted to evaluate the Mental Health Literacy (MHL) of primary schoolteachers regarding ADHD and to identify the predictors of MHL among teachers. Methods: A cross‐sectional, quantitative study was conducted among 303 primary schoolteachers. The study period was from December 2023 to June 2024. The study was conducted in Varkala and Attingal educational subdistricts of Thiruvananthapuram district. There are a total of 110 schools in both Attingal and Varkala subdistrict. A stratified cluster sampling technique was used to recruit the participants. Data were collected using a semi‐structured validated questionnaire. Furthermore, the Knowledge of Attention Deficit Disorder Scale (KADDSs) was used. Descriptive and inferential statistics were used to assess knowledge and compare across socio‐demographic characteristics. Binomial logistic regression was used to identify the predictors of MHL. Results: The majority of participants (56.8%) were in the age group of 35–50 years, with 94.4% being female. Most teachers (38%) had more than 10 years of teaching experience. The study found that 98.7% of teachers had a low level of knowledge related to ADHD. The mean knowledge scores showed significant association with socio‐demographic characteristics such as marital status (P = 0.01), status of work (P = 0.004), type of school (P < 0.001), teaching hours per week (P = 0.005), medium of instruction (P = 0.03) and average class strength (P < 0.001). The school area and average class strength were significant predictors influencing the MHL of teachers (adjusted odds ratio [AOR]: 3.40, 95% confidence interval [CI]: 1.22–9.44; AOR = 2.38, 95% CI: 1.37–4.15, respectively). Conclusion: Findings show that most teachers have low ADHD knowledge, influenced by socio‐demographic factors, with school area and class strength significantly predicting MHL. This highlights the need for targeted training to improve teachers’ ability to support students with ADHD. Email: ysy77@163.com Effects of Exercise on Hyperactivity/ Impulsivity and Inhibitory Control at Behavioral and Electrophysiological Levels in ADHD: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis Zeping Zhang1 , Xuanyu Bo2 , Kun Liu3 , Jiangdi Su1 , Yongfei Zhu4 , and Suyong Yang5 Abstract Objective: This study aimed to assess the impact of exercise on hyperactivity/impulsivity, inhibitory control, and inhibitionrelated event-related potential (ERP) components in individuals with ADHD. Method: A systematic search identified relevant studies, and methodological quality was assessed using the Revised Cochrane Risk-of-Bias tool for randomized trials (RoB 2) and the Risk of Bias in Non-Randomized Studies of Interventions (ROBINS-I), with data analysis conducted using Stata software. Results: A total of 36 studies (38 comparisons) were included, comprising 10 acute and 26 chronic exercise interventions. Subgroup analyses of inhibitory control revealed significant moderating effects of age (children/adolescents), intervention type (chronic interventions), frequency (three sessions per week), control condition (sedentary or no-intervention groups), and study quality (studies with moderate or high risk of bias). Conclusion: Exercise enhances inhibitory control in individuals with ADHD, with the effect being especially pronounced in children and adolescents. Chronic interventions and a frequency of three sessions per week appear to be most beneficial. However, it shows no significant effect on hyperactivity/impulsivity or inhibition-related N2 and P3 components. The impact of exercising should not be overestimated. (J. of Att. Optimizing exercise interventions for motor skills enhancement in attention deficit hyperactivity disorder: Evidence from meta-analysis Yuxuan Zhang1,*, Xiaoman Che1,*, Zhenghao Dong2, Jiaxing Shen1, Ye Tao3, Bo Cui4 and Feilong Zhu5 Abstract Objective: This meta-analysis aimed to evaluate effects of physical exercises on gross and fine motor skills in children and adolescents with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), and to explore the influencing factors and dose–response relationships for optimizing exercise parameters. Data sources: Six electronic databases were searched from inception to 20 June 2025, with an updated search performed on 27 January 2026. Methods: The effect sizes were estimated using Hedge’s g within three-level random-effects modeling frameworks. Subgroup analyses were performed to examine potential moderators, while restricted cubic splines were employed to characterize dose–response relationships. Results: Twenty-one studies with 850 participants were included. Physical exercises showed moderate positive effects on both gross motor skills (Hedge’s g = 0.62, 95% CI: 0.48–0.76) and fine motor skills (Hedge’s g = 0.72, 95% CI: 0.54–0.90). Significant improvements were observed in subcomponents of gross motor skills (locomotion, object control, body coordination, and strength) and fine motor skills (manual dexterity, hand-eye coordination, and handwriting). Moderator analyses indicated that longer session durations (>45 min) and higher frequencies (≥3 sessions/week) were associated with greater benefits. Fine motor skills (∼1350 min) appeared to require greater cumulative exercise exposure than gross motor skills (∼810 min) to achieve substantial gains. 1School of Clinical Medicine, Yangzhou Polytechnic University, Yangzhou, China 2The Affiliated Xuzhou Rehabilitation Hospital of Xuzhou Medical University, Xuzhou Rehabilitation Hospital, Xuzhou, China 3Department of Physical Education, Sejong University, Seoul, Korea 4Yangzhou Guangling Bai Xingtang Traditional Chinese Medicine Clinic, Yangzhou, China 5School of Sports Medicine and Rehabilitation, Beijing Sport University, Beijing, China *Yuxuan Zhang and Xiaoman Che contributed equally to this work and share first authorship. PDF_159 | öztürk-et-al-2026-increased-serum-netrin-1-levels-among-children-diagnosed-with-adhd.pdf | 8 pages | duplicate=None
https://doi.org/10.1177/10870547251408127 Journal of Attention Disorders 2026, Vol. 30(6) 765–772 © The Author(s) 2026 Article reuse guidelines: sagepub.com/journals-permissions DOI: 10.1177/10870547251408127 journals.sagepub.com/home/jad Article Introduction ADHD is one of the most common neurodevelopmental disorders of childhood characterized by hyperactivity, inattention, and impulsivity (Sharma & Couture, 2013). According to DSM-5 diagnostic criteria, the prevalence of ADHD is 11.3% in children under 12 years of age and 12.7% in adolescents aged 12 to 18 years (Salari et al., 2023). In a study conducted in Turkey, the prevalence of ADHD among primary school children was 12.7% (Ercan et al., 2015). Its etiopathogenesis has been reported to involve dopaminergic dysfunction, particularly in the mesocortical, mesolimbic, and nigrostriatal pathways (Del Campo et al., 2011; Sonuga-Barke, 2005). It is thought that dysfunction in the mesocortical dopamine pathway is associated with cognitive impairment, and that hypoactivity in the mesolimbic dopaminergic pathway plays a role in the motivational impairment seen in ADHD patients. The mesolimbic pathway is also an important part of the “reward” circuitry that is impaired in ADHD (Mehta et al., 2019). At the same time, the striatum is the largest source of dopamine in the brain, suggesting an important role for dopamine in attention. Connections to the striatum are a major source of dopaminergic synapses and have been suggested to play a role in ADHD (Dougherty et al., 1999). ADHD is increasingly seen as a syndrome of dysconnectivity rather than a disorder defined by changes in specific brain regions (Cortese et al., 2021). Study of functional connectivity in ADHD suggests that dopaminergic functional connectivity is a potential prognostic factor in ADHD (Zaher et al., 2024). It has also been demonstrated that ADHD patients exhibit significantly greater connectivity 1408127 JADXXX10.1177/10870547251408127Journal of Attention DisordersÖztürk et al. research-article2026 1Ankara University, Turkey 2Bayburt State Hospital, Turkey Corresponding Author: Hümeyra Hilal Öztürk, Department of Child and Adolescent Psychiatry, Bayburt State Hospital, 69000 Bayburt, Turkey. Email: hhilalozturk@gmail.com Increased Serum Netrin-1 Levels Among Children Diagnosed With ADHD Hümeyra Hilal Öztürk1,2 , Selen Sezen1 , Elçin Çağlar1 , Serenay Elgün Ülkar1 , and Sadettin Burak Açıkel1 Abstract Objective: ADHD is a common neurodevelopmental disorder of childhood characterized by altered projections of dopaminergic neurons and connectivity issues in various brain regions. In our study, we aim to investigate the potential effects of axon guidance molecules, Netrin-1 and Semaphorins 3A, 4D, and 7A, on these connectivity problems by examining the levels of these molecules in the peripheral blood of children with ADHD compared to healthy controls. Methods: A total of 43 children with ADHD and 40 healthy controls 6 to 12 years of age were included in the study. The K-SADS-PL was administered to exclude any additional psychopathologies (excluding ODD in the ADHD group). The Revised Child Anxiety and Depression Scale was provided to the children, while parents completed the revised Conners’ Parent Rating Scale (CPRS-R), the Social Responsiveness Scale (SRS), and the Behavior Rating Inventory for Executive Functioning (BRIEF). Additionally, teachers of the ADHD group were given the Conners’ Teacher Rating Scale. Furthermore, the WISC-4 was administered to assess the IQ profile of 35 children in the ADHD group. Results: Netrin-1 was found to be statistically higher in the ADHD group. When the R-CADS scores were recalculated taking the covariate into account, the significant increase in the ADHD group remained. There was no statistically significant difference between the two groups for semaphorin 3A, 4D, and 7A. The positive correlation of Netrin-1 with the hyperactivity subscores on the CPRS-R and the Global Executive Score on the BRIEF scale is noteworthy. Conclusion: Netrin-1 may play a role in the etiopathogenesis of ADHD. Further studies are needed to clarify the relationship between Netrin-1 and ADHD. (J. of Att.
